I haven’t really wanted to go to my dance class, and I used to like that. It’s not fancy dancing or anything, just the little class, but it got me out.\n\nAnd there are those small galleries I like to walk through sometimes—half the time I don’t even buy anything, I just look—but lately I don’t feel like going. Same with cooking Sunday food for my family. That usually makes me feel like myself, you know? But right now I just don’t have the pull toward it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling tired or lacking energy most days, even when you haven’t been doing much physically?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… tired, maybe sometimes, but I haven’t really been keeping track of it like that. I mean, at my age you sit down after lunch and you can feel a little slow, and then the phone rings about some leaking roof or somebody’s parking space, and you’re back up again.\n\nSo I can’t say it’s most days, exactly. I notice the thinking part more than the energy part.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or having a low mood most of the time, even when there’s nothing specific going on?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “down” most of the time. I mean, some days I’m just… kind of blah, but then I’ll get distracted by errands or something around the building, and I don’t sit there thinking, “Oh, I’m sad.”\n\nIt’s hard to say, because sometimes the day starts one way and then somebody calls about trash pickup or the elevator, and that becomes the whole mood of the day, you know? So… I’m not sure I can say it’s low most of the time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m trying to think… the past few weeks sort of run together for me. I can’t say there was a clear stretch where I was sad most days, even if things were fine. Some mornings feel heavier, maybe, but then I get caught up in whatever is going on.

equipment or start paperwork before I had really checked the budgets, and now I am worried I put us in a bad spot.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I am still getting to work, but I have had to slow down decisions, ask my assistant to double-check vendor details, and put off a couple of board items because I do not trust my judgment right now.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 weeks\n  manifestation: This sharper change in my energy, confidence, and then the low cloudy feeling has been going on for about two weeks, since my husband retired and was suddenly home all day.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 1 year\n  manifestation: The depressed stretches have been I haven’t noticed a clear run of days like that, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times when you felt down or low that seemed to come out of nowhere, not because of something that happened or something you were thinking about?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say it came out of nowhere. Yeah… I mean, those things usually do something for me. The dance class, even if I’m not the best one there, I like the music and seeing the ladies. And the little galleries, I used to stop in just to look, you know, even if I wasn’t buying anything.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not no pleasure at all, exactly… it’s more like I just don’t want to go. Like the dance class, I know I usually like the music and the people there, but getting myself there feels like too much, so I just don’t.

And the little galleries too — I used to wander in if I was nearby. There’s one with these tiny ceramic birds in the window, I always notice them, but lately I just keep walking. Same with Sunday cooking for my family. That usually makes me feel like myself, you know, but lately I look at all the pots and the shopping and I think… no, not today.

I'm Dr. [Your Name]. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I came in because my head just feels… cloudy lately. Like at work, I’ll be looking at a board packet or some vendor email, and I just sit there staring at it. I used to make those little decisions pretty fast, you know, that’s part of managing properties, but now even simple things feel hard to sort out.\n\nAnd those emails, sometimes they’re written so poorly anyway—half the vendors don’t even put the right address in the subject line—but still, I know it’s me too. I just can’t seem to think it through like I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice these thinking difficulties only at work, or do they also happen in other parts of your life, like at home or with friends?\n\nPatient: Mostly I notice it at work, because that’s where all the papers are, the board packets and the vendor emails and all that. I’ll be sitting there trying to decide something simple, something I used to just handle quick, and my head feels cloudy.\n\nI mean, those emails can be a mess anyway—people don’t write clear, and then I’m trying to figure out what building they even mean—but lately it feels like I just stare at it too long and can’t make the decision like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to think clearly or make decisions in other parts of your life, like at home or when you’re out with friends?\n\nPatient: Well… I notice it most at work, honestly, because that’s where I’m looking at those board packets and vendor emails. I’ll be staring at something simple, something I used to just decide on quick, and my head feels cloudy.\n\nAt home it’s not like I’m reading board minutes, you know, so it doesn’t stand out the same way. But the main thing bringing me in is that I can’t think through those simple work decisions like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you find yourself losing interest in things you usually enjoy, like hobbies or spending time with family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so. I haven’t really wanted to go to my dance class, and I used to like that. It’s not fancy dancing or anything, just the little class, but it got me out.\n\nAnd there are those small galleries I like to walk through sometimes—half the time I don’t even buy anything, I just look—but lately I don’t feel like going. Same with cooking Sunday food for my family. That usually makes me feel like myself, you know? But right now I just don’t have the pull toward it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling tired or lacking energy most days, even when you haven’t been doing much physically?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… tired, maybe sometimes, but I haven’t really been keeping track of it like that. I mean, at my age you sit down after lunch and you can feel a little slow, and then the phone rings about some leaking roof or somebody’s parking space, and you’re back up again.\n\nSo I can’t say it’s most days, exactly. I notice the thinking part more than the energy part.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or having a low mood most of the time, even when there’s nothing specific going on?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “down” most of the time. I mean, some days I’m just… kind of blah, but then I’ll get distracted by errands or something around the building, and I don’t sit there thinking, “Oh, I’m sad.”\n\nIt’s hard to say, because sometimes the day starts one way and then somebody calls about trash pickup or the elevator, and that becomes the whole mood of the day, you know? So… I’m not sure I can say it’s low most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Can you think back over the past few weeks—was there a time when you felt down or sad most days, even when things were going okay around you?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think… the past few weeks sort of run together for me. I can’t say there was a clear stretch where I was sad most days, even if things were fine. Some mornings feel heavier, maybe, but then I get caught up in whatever is going on.\n\nLike last week there was this whole thing with a neighbor saying the hallway light was flickering, and then I’m thinking about calling someone, and then I’m thinking about whether I paid the phone bill—so my mind goes all over. I don’t really sit and measure, “Was I sad today?” You know?\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed it in that clear way.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, have you had days where you felt low or sad even when nothing particular was wrong or bothering you?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe here and there, but I can’t really say it like that for sure. Sometimes I wake up kind of quiet inside, if that makes sense, but then the day gets going and I’m dealing with the building or a phone call, and I don’t know if it was sadness or just being tired of everything.\n\nNothing particular wrong… well, there’s always something small, isn’t there? A bill, a neighbor complaining, the laundry room acting up. So I have a hard time separating it out. I haven’t really noticed clear days where I was just sad for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you had mornings or parts of days where you felt a sort of emptiness or low mood even before anything stressful happened, like before a bill or a neighbor issue came up?\n\nPatient: Maybe… I don’t know. Some mornings I wake up and I’m not exactly cheerful, but I don’t know if I’d call it emptiness. I might just be lying there thinking I should get up, make coffee, check if anyone left a message.\n\nAnd then, you know, once I start thinking about the building, or whether the mail came, or if I forgot to call somebody back, it’s hard to remember what I felt before all that. It sort of blends together. So I can’t really say I’ve noticed that clearly, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you had several days in a row where you felt down or low most of the day, even after you've handled the usual things like calls or bills and there wasn't anything specific bothering you?\n\nPatient: Several days in a row… I don’t think I can really pin it down like that. Once I handle the calls or the bills, there’s usually something else in my head, even if it’s not a big thing.\n\nLike yesterday I thought I was done with everything, then I remembered the mail key was sticking, and then I was thinking about who I lent my step stool to. So by the time you ask if I was low most of the day, I kind of don’t know. I haven’t noticed a clear run of days like that, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times when you felt down or low that seemed to come out of nowhere, not because of something that happened or something you were thinking about?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say it came out of nowhere. Usually if I feel kind of low, there’s some thought attached to it, even if it’s a little thing. So I can’t really say I’ve noticed that clearly, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you had several days in a row where you felt down or low most of the day, even after you've handled the usual things like calls or bills and there wasn't anything specific bothering you?\n\nPatient: Several days in a row… I don’t think I can really pin it down like that. Once I handle the calls or the bills, there’s usually something else in my head, even if it’s not a big thing.\n\nLike yesterday I thought I was done with everything, then I remembered the mail key was sticking, and then I was thinking about who I lent my step stool to. So by the time you ask if I was low most of the day, I kind of don’t know. I haven’t noticed a clear run of days like that, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times when you felt down or low that seemed to come out of nowhere, not because of something that happened or something you were thinking about?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say it came out of nowhere. Usually if I feel kind of low, there’s some thought attached to it, even if it’s a little thing. Like I’ll remember I need to call someone about the hallway light, or I’ll think about how the grocery prices keep going up—my sister was just saying that too, she buys the same bread every week and it’s never the same price anymore.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed it just landing on me for no reason. It might happen, but I don’t catch it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that activities you used to enjoy, like your dance class or walking through galleries, no longer give you any pleasure at all, or do they still feel somewhat enjoyable even if you’re not as interested in doing them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, those things usually do something for me. I don’t know if it gives me no pleasure at all, but I haven’t had much pull toward it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that things you used to enjoy, like your dance class or cooking for your family, don’t feel pleasurable to you at all anymore?\n\nPatient: Not no pleasure at all, exactly… it’s more like I just don’t want to go. I wouldn’t say almost nothing… but the things that usually make me feel like me, I haven’t wanted to do them. My dance class, for one — I keep thinking I should go, and then I just don’t. And the little galleries, I used to like looking around, even if I was only killing time between errands.

I just can’t seem to think it through like I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice these thinking difficulties only at work, or do they also happen in other parts of your life, like at home or with friends?\n\nPatient: Mostly I notice it at work, because that’s where all the papers are, the board packets and the vendor emails and all that. I’ll be sitting there trying to decide something simple, something I used to just handle quick, and my head feels cloudy.\n\nI mean, those emails can be a mess anyway—people don’t write clear, and then I’m trying to figure out what building they even mean—but lately it feels like I just stare at it too long and can’t make the decision like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to think clearly or make decisions in other parts of your life, like at home or when you’re out with friends?\n\nPatient: Well… I notice it most at work, honestly, because that’s where I’m looking at those board packets and vendor emails. I’ll be staring at something simple, something I used to just decide on quick, and my head feels cloudy.\n\nAt home it’s not like I’m reading board minutes, you know, so it doesn’t stand out the same way. But the main thing bringing me in is that I can’t think through those simple work decisions like I used to.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you find yourself losing interest in things you usually enjoy, like hobbies or spending time with family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so. I haven’t really wanted to go to my dance class, and I used to like that. It’s not fancy dancing or anything, just the little class, but it got me out.\n\nAnd there are those small galleries I like to walk through sometimes—half the time I don’t even buy anything, I just look—but lately I don’t feel like going. Same with cooking Sunday food for my family. That usually makes me feel like myself, you know? But right now I just don’t have the pull toward it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling tired or lacking energy most days, even when you haven’t been doing much physically?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… tired, maybe sometimes, but I haven’t really been keeping track of it like that. I mean, at my age you sit down after lunch and you can feel a little slow, and then the phone rings about some leaking roof or somebody’s parking space, and you’re back up again.\n\nSo I can’t say it’s most days, exactly. I notice the thinking part more than the energy part.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or having a low mood most of the time, even when there’s nothing specific going on?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “down” most of the time. I mean, some days I’m just… kind of blah, but then I’ll get distracted by errands or something around the building, and I don’t sit there thinking, “Oh, I’m sad.”\n\nIt’s hard to say, because sometimes the day starts one way and then somebody calls about trash pickup or the elevator, and that becomes the whole mood of the day, you know? So… I’m not sure I can say it’s low most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Can you think back over the past few weeks—was there a time when you felt down or sad most days, even when things were going okay around you?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think… the past few weeks sort of run together for me. I can’t say there was a clear stretch where I was sad most days, even if things were fine. Some mornings feel heavier, maybe, but then I get caught up in whatever is going on.\n\nLike last week there was this whole thing with a neighbor saying the hallway light was flickering, and then I’m thinking about calling someone, and then I’m thinking about whether I paid the phone bill—so my mind goes all over. I don’t really sit and measure, “Was I sad today?” You know?\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed it in that clear way.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, have you had days where you felt low or sad even when nothing particular was wrong or bothering you?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe here and there, but I can’t really say it like that for sure. Sometimes I wake up kind of quiet inside, if that makes sense, but then the day gets going and I’m dealing with the building or a phone call, and I don’t know if it was sadness or just being tired of everything.\n\nNothing particular wrong… well, there’s always something small, isn’t there? A bill, a neighbor complaining, the laundry room acting up. So I have a hard time separating it out. I haven’t really noticed clear days where I was just sad for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you had mornings or parts of days where you felt a sort of emptiness or low mood even before anything stressful happened, like before a bill or a neighbor issue came up?\n\nPatient: Maybe… I don’t know. Some mornings I wake up and I’m not exactly cheerful, but I don’t know if I’d call it emptiness. I might just be lying there thinking I should get up, make coffee, check if anyone left a message.\n\nAnd then, you know, once I start thinking about the building, or whether the mail came, or if I forgot to call somebody back, it’s hard to remember what I felt before all that. It sort of blends together. So I can’t really say I’ve noticed that clearly, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you had several days in a row where you felt down or low most of the day, even after you've handled the usual things like calls or bills and there wasn't anything specific bothering you?\n\nPatient: Several days in a row… I don’t think I can really pin it down like that. Once I handle the calls or the bills, there’s usually something else in my head, even if it’s not a big thing.\n\nLike yesterday I thought I was done with everything, then I remembered the mail key was sticking, and then I was thinking about who I lent my step stool to. So by the time you ask if I was low most of the day, I kind of don’t know. I haven’t noticed a clear run of days like that, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times when you felt down or low that seemed to come out of nowhere, not because of something that happened or something you were thinking about?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say it came out of nowhere. Usually if I feel kind of low, there’s some thought attached to it, even if it’s a little thing. Like I’ll remember I need to call someone about the hallway light, or I’ll think about how the grocery prices keep going up—my sister was just saying that too, she buys the same bread every week and it’s never the same price anymore.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed it just landing on me for no reason. It might happen, but I don’t catch it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that activities you used to enjoy, like your dance class or walking through galleries, no longer give you any pleasure at all, or do they still feel somewhat enjoyable even if you’re not as interested in doing them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, those things usually do something for me. The dance class, even if I’m not the best one there, I like the music and seeing the ladies. And the little galleries, I used to stop in just to look, you know, even if I wasn’t buying anything.\n\nLately I just haven’t wanted to go. Same with making Sunday food for my family — that usually makes me feel like myself, getting everything going in the kitchen. But I look at it now and think, eh, maybe not. I don’t know if it gives me no pleasure at all, but I haven’t had much pull toward it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that things you used to enjoy, like your dance class or cooking for your family, don’t feel pleasurable to you at all anymore?\n\nPatient: Not no pleasure at all, exactly… it’s more like I just don’t want to go. Like the dance class, I know I usually like the music and the people there, but getting myself there feels like too much, so I just don’t.\n\nAnd the little galleries too — I used to wander in if I was nearby. There’s one with these tiny ceramic birds in the window, I always notice them, but lately I just keep walking. Same with Sunday cooking for my family. That usually makes me feel like myself, you know, but lately I look at all the pots and the shopping and I think… no, not today.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that almost nothing gives you pleasure anymore, even things you used to really enjoy?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say almost nothing… but the things that usually make me feel like me, I haven’t wanted to do them. My dance class, for one — I keep thinking I should go, and then I just don’t. And the little galleries, I used to like looking around, even if I was only killing time between errands.\n\nSunday cooking too… that’s a big one. My family expects it, and I used to like having everybody fed and fussing over the pots, but lately I just don’t feel like starting. I still know those things are nice, I just don’t seem to reach for them.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Not any pleasure at all… I don’t know if I’d put it that strong. It’s more that I haven’t wanted to do them. The dance class, the little galleries I used to peek into, Sunday cooking for my family — those are things that usually make me feel like myself.

I might just be lying there thinking I should get up, make coffee, check if anyone left a message.\n\nAnd then, you know, once I start thinking about the building, or whether the mail came, or if I forgot to call somebody back, it’s hard to remember what I felt before all that. It sort of blends together. So I can’t really say I’ve noticed that clearly, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you had several days in a row where you felt down or low most of the day, even after you've handled the usual things like calls or bills and there wasn't anything specific bothering you?\n\nPatient: Several days in a row… I don’t think I can really pin it down like that. Once I handle the calls or the bills, there’s usually something else in my head, even if it’s not a big thing.\n\nLike yesterday I thought I was done with everything, then I remembered the mail key was sticking, and then I was thinking about who I lent my step stool to. So by the time you ask if I was low most of the day, I kind of don’t know. I haven’t noticed a clear run of days like that, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times when you felt down or low that seemed to come out of nowhere, not because of something that happened or something you were thinking about?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say it came out of nowhere. I don’t know if it gives me no pleasure at all, but I haven’t had much pull toward it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that things you used to enjoy, like your dance class or cooking for your family, don’t feel pleasurable to you at all anymore?\n\nPatient: Not no pleasure at all, exactly… it’s more like I just don’t want to go. There’s one with these tiny ceramic birds in the window, I always notice them, but lately I just keep walking. Same with Sunday cooking for my family. That usually makes me feel like myself, you know, but lately I look at all the pots and the shopping and I think… no, not today.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that almost nothing gives you pleasure anymore, even things you used to really enjoy?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say almost nothing… but the things that usually make me feel like me, I haven’t wanted to do them. I still know those things are nice, I just don’t seem to reach for them.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that even the things you used to really enjoy, like your dance class or cooking for your family, don’t give you any pleasure at all anymore?\n\nPatient: Not any pleasure at all… I don’t know if I’d put it that strong. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think they’d probably still feel a little enjoyable if I got myself there, maybe… it’s not like I hate them now. I just haven’t wanted to go. The dance class, those little galleries with all the pretty things in the windows, and the Sunday food for my family — those are usually the things that make me feel like myself.

But lately I look at it all and it feels like too much trouble to start. Like with cooking, you know, you have to get the meat, the vegetables, make sure somebody didn’t use the last onion — and then I end up thinking, oh, forget it. So yes, I guess it’s more not wanting to do them than them giving me no pleasure at all.

I mean, at my age you sit down after lunch and you can feel a little slow, and then the phone rings about some leaking roof or somebody’s parking space, and you’re back up again.\n\nSo I can’t say it’s most days, exactly. I notice the thinking part more than the energy part.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or having a low mood most of the time, even when there’s nothing specific going on?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “down” most of the time. I mean, some days I’m just… kind of blah, but then I’ll get distracted by errands or something around the building, and I don’t sit there thinking, “Oh, I’m sad.”\n\nIt’s hard to say, because sometimes the day starts one way and then somebody calls about trash pickup or the elevator, and that becomes the whole mood of the day, you know? So… I’m not sure I can say it’s low most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Can you think back over the past few weeks—was there a time when you felt down or sad most days, even when things were going okay around you?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think… the past few weeks sort of run together for me. I can’t say there was a clear stretch where I was sad most days, even if things were fine. Some mornings feel heavier, maybe, but then I get caught up in whatever is going on.\n\nLike last week there was this whole thing with a neighbor saying the hallway light was flickering, and then I’m thinking about calling someone, and then I’m thinking about whether I paid the phone bill—so my mind goes all over. I don’t really sit and measure, “Was I sad today?” You know?\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed it in that clear way.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, have you had days where you felt low or sad even when nothing particular was wrong or bothering you?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe here and there, but I can’t really say it like that for sure. Sometimes I wake up kind of quiet inside, if that makes sense, but then the day gets going and I’m dealing with the building or a phone call, and I don’t know if it was sadness or just being tired of everything.\n\nNothing particular wrong… well, there’s always something small, isn’t there? A bill, a neighbor complaining, the laundry room acting up. So I have a hard time separating it out. I haven’t really noticed clear days where I was just sad for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you had mornings or parts of days where you felt a sort of emptiness or low mood even before anything stressful happened, like before a bill or a neighbor issue came up?\n\nPatient: Maybe… I don’t know. Some mornings I wake up and I’m not exactly cheerful, but I don’t know if I’d call it emptiness. I might just be lying there thinking I should get up, make coffee, check if anyone left a message.\n\nAnd then, you know, once I start thinking about the building, or whether the mail came, or if I forgot to call somebody back, it’s hard to remember what I felt before all that. It sort of blends together. So I can’t really say I’ve noticed that clearly, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you had several days in a row where you felt down or low most of the day, even after you've handled the usual things like calls or bills and there wasn't anything specific bothering you?\n\nPatient: Several days in a row… I don’t think I can really pin it down like that. Once I handle the calls or the bills, there’s usually something else in my head, even if it’s not a big thing.\n\nLike yesterday I thought I was done with everything, then I remembered the mail key was sticking, and then I was thinking about who I lent my step stool to. So by the time you ask if I was low most of the day, I kind of don’t know. I haven’t noticed a clear run of days like that, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times when you felt down or low that seemed to come out of nowhere, not because of something that happened or something you were thinking about?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say it came out of nowhere. Usually if I feel kind of low, there’s some thought attached to it, even if it’s a little thing. Like I’ll remember I need to call someone about the hallway light, or I’ll think about how the grocery prices keep going up—my sister was just saying that too, she buys the same bread every week and it’s never the same price anymore.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed it just landing on me for no reason. It sort of blends together. Some mornings feel heavier, maybe, but then I get caught up in whatever is going on.\n\nLike last week there was this whole thing with a neighbor saying the hallway light was flickering, and then I’m thinking about calling someone, and then I’m thinking about whether I paid the phone bill—so my mind goes all over. I don’t really sit and measure, “Was I sad today?” You know?\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed it in that clear way.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, have you had days where you felt low or sad even when nothing particular was wrong or bothering you?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe here and there, but I can’t really say it like that for sure. Sometimes I wake up kind of quiet inside, if that makes sense, but then the day gets going and I’m dealing with the building or a phone call, and I don’t know if it was sadness or just being tired of everything.\n\nNothing particular wrong… well, there’s always something small, isn’t there? A bill, a neighbor complaining, the laundry room acting up. So I have a hard time separating it out. I haven’t really noticed clear days where I was just sad for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you had mornings or parts of days where you felt a sort of emptiness or low mood even before anything stressful happened, like before a bill or a neighbor issue came up?\n\nPatient: Maybe… I don’t know. Some mornings I wake up and I’m not exactly cheerful, but I don’t know if I’d call it emptiness. I might just be lying there thinking I should get up, make coffee, check if anyone left a message.\n\nAnd then, you know, once I start thinking about the building, or whether the mail came, or if I forgot to call somebody back, it’s hard to remember what I felt before all that. It sort of blends together. So I can’t really say I’ve noticed that clearly, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you had several days in a row where you felt down or low most of the day, even after you've handled the usual things like calls or bills and there wasn't anything specific bothering you?\n\nPatient: Several days in a row… I don’t think I can really pin it down like that. Once I handle the calls or the bills, there’s usually something else in my head, even if it’s not a big thing.\n\nLike yesterday I thought I was done with everything, then I remembered the mail key was sticking, and then I was thinking about who I lent my step stool to. So by the time you ask if I was low most of the day, I kind of don’t know. I haven’t noticed a clear run of days like that, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times when you felt down or low that seemed to come out of nowhere, not because of something that happened or something you were thinking about?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say it came out of nowhere. Usually if I feel kind of low, there’s some thought attached to it, even if it’s a little thing. Like I’ll remember I need to call someone about the hallway light, or I’ll think about how the grocery prices keep going up—my sister was just saying that too, she buys the same bread every week and it’s never the same price anymore.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed it just landing on me for no reason. It might happen, but I don’t catch it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that activities you used to enjoy, like your dance class or walking through galleries, no longer give you any pleasure at all, or do they still feel somewhat enjoyable even if you’re not as interested in doing them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, those things usually do something for me. The dance class, even if I’m not the best one there, I like the music and seeing the ladies. And the little galleries, I used to stop in just to look, you know, even if I wasn’t buying anything.\n\nLately I just haven’t wanted to go. Same with making Sunday food for my family — that usually makes me feel like myself, getting everything going in the kitchen. But I look at it now and think, eh, maybe not. I don’t know if it gives me no pleasure at all, but I haven’t had much pull toward it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that things you used to enjoy, like your dance class or cooking for your family, don’t feel pleasurable to you at all anymore?\n\nPatient: Not no pleasure at all, exactly… it’s more like I just don’t want to go. Like the dance class, I know I usually like the music and the people there, but getting myself there feels like too much, so I just don’t.\n\nAnd the little galleries too — I used to wander in if I was nearby. There’s one with these tiny ceramic birds in the window, I always notice them, but lately I just keep walking. Same with Sunday cooking for my family. That usually makes me feel like myself, you know, but lately I look at all the pots and the shopping and I think… no, not today.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that almost nothing gives you pleasure anymore, even things you used to really enjoy?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say almost nothing… but the things that usually make me feel like me, I haven’t wanted to do them. My dance class, for one — I keep thinking I should go, and then I just don’t. And the little galleries, I used to like looking around, even if I was only killing time between errands.\n\nSunday cooking too… that’s a big one. My family expects it, and I used to like having everybody fed and fussing over the pots, but lately I just don’t feel like starting. I still know those things are nice, I just don’t seem to reach for them.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that even the things you used to really enjoy, like your dance class or cooking for your family, don’t give you any pleasure at all anymore?\n\nPatient: Not any pleasure at all… I don’t know if I’d put it that strong. It’s more that I haven’t wanted to do them. The dance class, the little galleries I used to peek into, Sunday cooking for my family — those are things that usually make me feel like myself.\n\nAnd you know, with the cooking, once you start chopping onions and getting the big pot out, it used to kind of carry me along. My aunt used to say you can tell a house by what’s simmering in it… but lately I just look at it and think, no, I’m not doing all that. Same with the class. I just don’t go.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that things you used to enjoy, like your dance class or cooking for your family, don’t give you any pleasure anymore, or do they still feel at least a little enjoyable even if you don’t feel like doing them?\n\nPatient: I think they’d probably still feel a little enjoyable if I got myself there, maybe… it’s not like I hate them now. I just haven’t wanted to go. The dance class, those little galleries with all the pretty things in the windows, and the Sunday food for my family — those are usually the things that make me feel like myself.\n\nBut lately I look at it all and it feels like too much trouble to start. It sort of blends together. So I can’t really say I’ve noticed that clearly, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you had several days in a row where you felt down or low most of the day, even after you've handled the usual things like calls or bills and there wasn't anything specific bothering you?\n\nPatient: Several days in a row… I don’t think I can really pin it down like that. Once I handle the calls or the bills, there’s usually something else in my head, even if it’s not a big thing.\n\nLike yesterday I thought I was done with everything, then I remembered the mail key was sticking, and then I was thinking about who I lent my step stool to. So by the time you ask if I was low most of the day, I kind of don’t know. I haven’t noticed a clear run of days like that, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times when you felt down or low that seemed to come out of nowhere, not because of something that happened or something you were thinking about?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say it came out of nowhere. Usually if I feel kind of low, there’s some thought attached to it, even if it’s a little thing. Like I’ll remember I need to call someone about the hallway light, or I’ll think about how the grocery prices keep going up—my sister was just saying that too, she buys the same bread every week and it’s never the same price anymore.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed it just landing on me for no reason. It might happen, but I don’t catch it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that activities you used to enjoy, like your dance class or walking through galleries, no longer give you any pleasure at all, or do they still feel somewhat enjoyable even if you’re not as interested in doing them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, those things usually do something for me. The dance class, even if I’m not the best one there, I like the music and seeing the ladies. And the little galleries, I used to stop in just to look, you know, even if I wasn’t buying anything.\n\nLately I just haven’t wanted to go. Same with making Sunday food for my family — that usually makes me feel like myself, getting everything going in the kitchen. But I look at it now and think, eh, maybe not. I don’t know if it gives me no pleasure at all, but I haven’t had much pull toward it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that things you used to enjoy, like your dance class or cooking for your family, don’t feel pleasurable to you at all anymore?\n\nPatient: Not no pleasure at all, exactly… it’s more like I just don’t want to go. Like the dance class, I know I usually like the music and the people there, but getting myself there feels like too much, so I just don’t.\n\nAnd the little galleries too — I used to wander in if I was nearby. There’s one with these tiny ceramic birds in the window, I always notice them, but lately I just keep walking. Same with Sunday cooking for my family. That usually makes me feel like myself, you know, but lately I look at all the pots and the shopping and I think… no, not today.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that almost nothing gives you pleasure anymore, even things you used to really enjoy?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say almost nothing… but the things that usually make me feel like me, I haven’t wanted to do them. My dance class, for one — I keep thinking I should go, and then I just don’t. And the little galleries, I used to like looking around, even if I was only killing time between errands.\n\nSunday cooking too… that’s a big one. My family expects it, and I used to like having everybody fed and fussing over the pots, but lately I just don’t feel like starting. I still know those things are nice, I just don’t seem to reach for them.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that even the things you used to really enjoy, like your dance class or cooking for your family, don’t give you any pleasure at all anymore?\n\nPatient: Not any pleasure at all… I don’t know if I’d put it that strong. It’s more that I haven’t wanted to do them. The dance class, the little galleries I used to peek into, Sunday cooking for my family — those are things that usually make me feel like myself.\n\nAnd you know, with the cooking, once you start chopping onions and getting the big pot out, it used to kind of carry me along. My aunt used to say you can tell a house by what’s simmering in it… but lately I just look at it and think, no, I’m not doing all that. Same with the class. I just don’t go.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that things you used to enjoy, like your dance class or cooking for your family, don’t give you any pleasure anymore, or do they still feel at least a little enjoyable even if you don’t feel like doing them?\n\nPatient: I think they’d probably still feel a little enjoyable if I got myself there, maybe… it’s not like I hate them now. I just haven’t wanted to go. The dance class, those little galleries with all the pretty things in the windows, and the Sunday food for my family — those are usually the things that make me feel like myself.\n\nBut lately I look at it all and it feels like too much trouble to start. Like with cooking, you know, you have to get the meat, the vegetables, make sure somebody didn’t use the last onion — and then I end up thinking, oh, forget it. So yes, I guess it’s more not wanting to do them than them giving me no pleasure at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you had several days in a row where you felt down or low most of the day, even after you've handled the usual things like calls or bills and there wasn't anything specific bothering you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, no. I mean, I have days where I’m tired of the phone ringing or dealing with bills—everybody wants something, you know? In my line of work there’s always somebody upset about a gate or a leak or a parking space, and then I come home and there’s still mail on the table.\n\nBut low most of the day for several days when nothing’s really wrong… I don’t know. I haven’t been keeping track like that. I guess I notice more that I don’t feel like doing much, but I can’t say it’s been that exact thing.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had days where you felt down or low most of the day, nearly every day, even after taking care of the usual things at work and home?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say nearly every day. I don’t really mark it on a calendar. I notice more that I just sort of keep moving through what has to be done.
